I’ll think about my family and whether they’re going to see me as someone who can’t handle things, or like I made all these choices and now I’m failing at them. It’s embarrassing because sometimes the thing that starts it is really small, but in my head it feels like proof that everything is on the edge of derailing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is something you can’t control, even when you try to stop thinking about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very much. That’s actually one of the most frustrating parts, because I’ll be aware that I’m doing it. Like I’ll literally tell myself, “Okay, stop. Just focus on the one thing in front of you.” But it doesn’t feel like my brain listens.\n\nI’ll start with one worry, like a work deadline, and then almost immediately it turns into, “Wait, what about that thing on my calendar?” So I check my calendar. Then while I’m checking that, I remember something about money or tuition or my budget, so I open that too. And then somehow I’m thinking about assignments, and whether I missed something there, so I go check that again.\n\nIt becomes this loop where I’m not even solving anything anymore. I’m just checking the same things over and over—calendar, budget, assignments, work stuff—and I keep thinking if I check one more time I’ll feel settled, but I don’t. I just lose a ton of time and then realize I haven’t actually gotten anything done, which makes me worry even more. So yes, it really does feel out of my control.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling this worry or anxiety, do you notice any physical symptoms, like a racing heart, sweating, shaking, or feeling tense?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I know I feel anxious, but I don’t always know how to separate what’s “physical” from just… being worked up in my head, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t think I’ve really noticed sweating or shaking in a clear way. And I’m not sure about my heart racing—I don’t usually stop and check my pulse or anything. Sometimes I’m so focused on the worry itself that I only realize afterward that I’ve been kind of bracing myself, but I don’t know if that counts as feeling tense or if I’m just stressed.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I haven’t paid close enough attention to say for sure. I mostly notice the thoughts and the inability to settle down more than specific body symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or unable to relax, even when there’s no specific reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly, because I don’t always know what counts as “no specific reason.” Like, if I’m restless, my mind usually finds a reason pretty quickly, even if it’s kind of vague or not actually urgent.\n\nI do have times where I feel like I should be able to just sit down and relax, and I can’t really settle into it. But I don’t know if that’s because there’s truly nothing to worry about, or because I’m already sort of keyed up and then I start looking for something that explains it. Like I’ll sit on the couch and think, “Okay, I’m done for the day, I can just watch something,” but then I’m still kind of… mentally hovering, I guess. Not necessarily pacing around or anything dramatic, just not fully relaxed.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I’ve noticed trouble relaxing, but I’m not totally sure whether it happens without a reason, because it feels like there’s almost always some possible reason my brain can attach to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have trouble concentrating or focusing on tasks because your mind is preoccupied with worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the main ways it messes with me day to day. I’ll be trying to do something that should be pretty straightforward, like working on a financial model for work, and I’ll just… freeze on the same part. I’m looking at the numbers, I know technically what I’m supposed to be doing, but my mind is already somewhere else running through every possible thing that could go wrong.\n\nOr with class readings, I’ll read the same paragraph over and over—like five times—and then realize I have no idea what it said. Not because it’s too hard, necessarily, but because while my eyes are moving across the page, my brain is thinking about whether I missed something, or if I’m behind, or if one thing going badly is going to make everything else fall apart.\n\nIt’s frustrating because from the outside it probably just looks like I’m being inefficient or distracted, but internally it feels like I’m trying to concentrate through this constant background noise. I’ll keep dragging myself back to the task, and then a minute later I’m off in my head again, going through all these scenarios. So yes, my focus is pretty badly affected.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you feel fatigued or low on energy most days, even if you’ve had some sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually been happening a lot. By the middle of the afternoon at work, I can feel just completely wiped out, like I hit a wall. And it’s not just normal “I could use a coffee” tired — it’s more like even small things start feeling weirdly effortful. Answering emails, switching between tasks, getting up to go to the bathroom, even thinking about walking to the subway after work can feel like this huge thing I have to gear myself up for.\n\nAnd I do sleep some, so it’s not like I’m awake all night every night or something. But even when I’ve slept, I don’t really feel restored. I keep thinking, “Okay, if I just make it to the weekend and rest, I’ll reset,” but then the weekend comes and I’ll rest, or try to, and by Monday I don’t feel like I’ve gotten my energy back in any meaningful way. It’s frustrating because I feel like I’m constantly running on a low battery, and then the regular parts of the day take way more out of me than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming more irritable or easily annoyed, especially when you're feeling tired or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, I can get annoyed like anyone else, but I don’t know that I’ve specifically noticed a big change where I’m suddenly snapping at people or anything like that.\n\nWhen I’m tired or overwhelmed, I think I’m more… short internally, if that makes sense. Like things feel harder to tolerate, and I may have less patience for interruptions or unexpected requests. But I don’t know if other people would describe me as irritable, because I usually try pretty hard to keep it contained and not show it. Sometimes I’ll notice myself thinking, “Why is this person asking me this right now?” when it’s probably a totally normal question.\n\nSo maybe there’s some of that, but I wouldn’t say it’s the main thing I’ve noticed, or at least I haven’t paid as much attention to it as I have to the tiredness and trouble focusing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about a wide range of things, even when they’re not necessarily that important or urgent?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not even like there’s one huge crisis I’m responding to — it’s more like my mind is constantly scanning for things to be worried about, and it jumps around between them all day.\n\nLike, I’ll start off worrying that I’m falling behind at work, even if I’m technically meeting deadlines, because I’ll think, “What if I’m missing something obvious? What if someone realizes I’m not doing enough?” And then somehow that turns into worrying about school, like whether I’m wasting money on it or whether I made the wrong decision, and then that turns into worrying about my family thinking I can’t handle things or that I’m not as put together as I should be at 29.\n\nAnd the thing is, a lot of these things are not urgent in the moment. Sometimes they’re not even actual problems yet. But they feel urgent in my head. Like a small issue doesn’t stay small — if I make one mistake on something, my brain immediately treats it like it could set off this whole chain reaction and derail my entire future. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real, like I have to solve everything right then or I’m going to fall apart somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even for short periods during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think hopeless is the word I’d use, at least not in a clear way. I do have moments where I feel kind of discouraged or flat, especially if I’ve been thinking too much about everything I need to handle, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as feeling depressed, exactly.\n\nAs far as losing interest in things, I mean… sometimes I don’t feel like doing much, but it’s hard for me to tell whether that’s because I’ve lost interest or because I’m just tired and mentally drained. Like if a friend suggests something after work, I might think, “That sounds like too much,” even if normally I’d enjoy seeing them. But I don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things, because if I actually get myself there, I can sometimes still have an okay time.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a strong pattern of feeling hopeless or not caring about anything. It’s more that my mood can dip for a bit when I’m overwhelmed, but I’m not sure I’d call it that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you take less pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for short periods during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I’d say “less pleasure,” exactly. I’ve thought about that a little because it’s hard to separate enjoyment from just being tired or preoccupied.\n\nLike, there are things I normally like — watching a show, going out to eat with a friend, doing something low-key after work — and sometimes they don’t sound appealing in the moment. But it’s not always because I don’t enjoy them anymore. It’s more like the effort of starting feels like a lot, or I’m already mentally somewhere else, so I’m not as excited about it as I think I should be.\n\nIf I actually do the thing, I can still enjoy it at least somewhat. Maybe not always fully, because my mind can be distracted, but I don’t think I’ve had this clear sense of, “Nothing is enjoyable anymore.” It’s more inconsistent than that. Some moments I’m engaged and fine, and other times I’m just sort of going through the motions because I’m worn out. So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a definite loss of pleasure, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel down or sad most of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going on to explain it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of being sad most of the day for no clear reason. I mean, I do have times where I feel kind of low or emotionally worn down, but usually when I really think about it, it’s tied to feeling overwhelmed or like I’ve been carrying too much in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up and the whole day just feels sad for no reason, if that makes sense. More often it’s like my mood gets pulled down after I’ve been thinking about responsibilities or things I’m worried I’m not handling well. And then I might feel discouraged for a while, or quieter than usual, or like I don’t have much energy to be upbeat. But I wouldn’t say it’s most of the day, and I wouldn’t say it comes out of nowhere.\n\nI guess I’m a little unsure where the line is, because sometimes the reason isn’t one obvious event — it’s more like a buildup. But no, I haven’t really noticed days where I’m just sad most of the time without something behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling asleep because your thoughts are racing or you can't shut them off, even when you're physically tired?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll be physically exhausted — like, I can feel that my body is tired — and I’ll get into bed in my apartment, close my laptop, lights off or mostly off, and then my brain just keeps going. It’s not even always one big dramatic thought. It’s more like I start mentally rechecking everything: work deadlines, emails I might have missed, whether I handled something correctly, then tuition payments, then class assignments, then I remember some other thing I need to do, and it just keeps looping.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ll tell myself, “Okay, you’re done for the day, you need to sleep,” but it doesn’t feel like there’s an off switch. I can be lying there for hours, not doing anything, not even on my computer anymore, but still kind of working in my head. And then even when I do finally fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up feeling like I never really turned off, like my mind was still running in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any tightness in your muscles, especially in your neck, shoulders, or back, even when you're not doing anything physically strenuous?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. But I don’t know if I’d call it muscle tightness as a symptom, exactly, because I also sit at a computer a lot for work, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just from being at my desk versus something else.\n\nMy neck and shoulders can feel tense at times, but I haven’t had this clear ongoing back or neck pain that I’ve been focused on. It’s more like I notice it after the fact, like, “Oh, I’m not relaxed,” but I wouldn’t say I’ve been doing anything physically strenuous when it happens. I just haven’t paid enough attention to say how often it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling asleep because your thoughts are racing or you can't shut them off, even when you're physically tired?\n\nPatient: Yes — definitely. It’s like I can’t convince my brain that it’s allowed to stop.\n\nSometimes I’ll be there for hours, not even doing anything, just lying there thinking through the same things again and again. It doesn’t feel restful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively sleepy or struggle to stay awake during the day, even after what you think should have been a full night's sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Yes, I do. It’s not even always that I don’t care about the task — like, I know the email needs an answer, or I know I need to start something for work — but by midafternoon at the office I feel so completely drained that starting feels weirdly impossible.

Even small things can feel like they require this huge amount of effort. I’ll be sitting there looking at my inbox thinking, “Okay, just answer this one message,” and it still feels like I have to force myself through mud to begin. And then by the time I’m leaving work, even something basic like walking to the subway can feel like more effort than it should.

I keep hoping that if I rest over the weekend, I’ll sort of reset and come back with more energy, but it doesn’t really work that way. I might take it easy, but I don’t feel restored enough. It’s like I’m starting from empty again.

It’s more that my mood can dip for a bit when I’m overwhelmed, but I’m not sure I’d call it that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you take less pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for short periods during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I’d say “less pleasure,” exactly. I’ve thought about that a little because it’s hard to separate enjoyment from just being tired or preoccupied.\n\nLike, there are things I normally like — watching a show, going out to eat with a friend, doing something low-key after work — and sometimes they don’t sound appealing in the moment. But it’s not always because I don’t enjoy them anymore. It’s more like the effort of starting feels like a lot, or I’m already mentally somewhere else, so I’m not as excited about it as I think I should be.\n\nIf I actually do the thing, I can still enjoy it at least somewhat. Maybe not always fully, because my mind can be distracted, but I don’t think I’ve had this clear sense of, “Nothing is enjoyable anymore.” It’s more inconsistent than that. Some moments I’m engaged and fine, and other times I’m just sort of going through the motions because I’m worn out. So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a definite loss of pleasure, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel down or sad most of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going on to explain it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of being sad most of the day for no clear reason. I mean, I do have times where I feel kind of low or emotionally worn down, but usually when I really think about it, it’s tied to feeling overwhelmed or like I’ve been carrying too much in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up and the whole day just feels sad for no reason, if that makes sense. More often it’s like my mood gets pulled down after I’ve been thinking about responsibilities or things I’m worried I’m not handling well. And then I might feel discouraged for a while, or quieter than usual, or like I don’t have much energy to be upbeat. But I wouldn’t say it’s most of the day, and I wouldn’t say it comes out of nowhere.\n\nI guess I’m a little unsure where the line is, because sometimes the reason isn’t one obvious event — it’s more like a buildup. But no, I haven’t really noticed days where I’m just sad most of the time without something behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling asleep because your thoughts are racing or you can't shut them off, even when you're physically tired?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll be physically exhausted — like, I can feel that my body is tired — and I’ll get into bed in my apartment, close my laptop, lights off or mostly off, and then my brain just keeps going. It’s not even always one big dramatic thought. It’s more like I start mentally rechecking everything: work deadlines, emails I might have missed, whether I handled something correctly, then tuition payments, then class assignments, then I remember some other thing I need to do, and it just keeps looping.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ll tell myself, “Okay, you’re done for the day, you need to sleep,” but it doesn’t feel like there’s an off switch. I can be lying there for hours, not doing anything, not even on my computer anymore, but still kind of working in my head. And then even when I do finally fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up feeling like I never really turned off, like my mind was still running in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any tightness in your muscles, especially in your neck, shoulders, or back, even when you're not doing anything physically strenuous?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Yes — mostly the thinking clearly and making decisions part. I’m not sure I’d say I’m physically moving in slow motion all the time, but mentally I get really stuck.

Like I’ll be looking at the same financial model at work, something I technically know how to review, and I’ll just… stare at it. I’ll read the same numbers or assumptions over and over and realize I haven’t actually taken anything in. Or with class readings, I’ll get through a paragraph and then notice I’ve basically reread the same paragraph five times and still couldn’t tell you what it said.

And it’s not because the material is impossible. It’s more like my mind is already somewhere else, running through everything that could go wrong — what if I miss something in the model, what if I fall behind, what if I don’t understand the reading well enough — and then I’m technically looking at the task, but I’m not really there. Even small decisions can take way longer than they should because I keep second-guessing what the “right” choice is.

I’ll keep dragging myself back to the task, and then a minute later I’m off in my head again, going through all these scenarios. Answering emails, switching between tasks, getting up to go to the bathroom, even thinking about walking to the subway after work can feel like this huge thing I have to gear myself up for.\n\nAnd I do sleep some, so it’s not like I’m awake all night every night or something. But even when I’ve slept, I don’t really feel restored. I keep thinking, “Okay, if I just make it to the weekend and rest, I’ll reset,” but then the weekend comes and I’ll rest, or try to, and by Monday I don’t feel like I’ve gotten my energy back in any meaningful way. It’s frustrating because I feel like I’m constantly running on a low battery, and then the regular parts of the day take way more out of me than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming more irritable or easily annoyed, especially when you're feeling tired or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, I can get annoyed like anyone else, but I don’t know that I’ve specifically noticed a big change where I’m suddenly snapping at people or anything like that.\n\nWhen I’m tired or overwhelmed, I think I’m more… short internally, if that makes sense. Like things feel harder to tolerate, and I may have less patience for interruptions or unexpected requests. But I don’t know if other people would describe me as irritable, because I usually try pretty hard to keep it contained and not show it. Sometimes I’ll notice myself thinking, “Why is this person asking me this right now?” when it’s probably a totally normal question.\n\nSo maybe there’s some of that, but I wouldn’t say it’s the main thing I’ve noticed, or at least I haven’t paid as much attention to it as I have to the tiredness and trouble focusing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry about a wide range of things, even when they’re not necessarily that important or urgent?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not even like there’s one huge crisis I’m responding to — it’s more like my mind is constantly scanning for things to be worried about, and it jumps around between them all day.\n\nLike, I’ll start off worrying that I’m falling behind at work, even if I’m technically meeting deadlines, because I’ll think, “What if I’m missing something obvious? What if someone realizes I’m not doing enough?” And then somehow that turns into worrying about school, like whether I’m wasting money on it or whether I made the wrong decision, and then that turns into worrying about my family thinking I can’t handle things or that I’m not as put together as I should be at 29.\n\nAnd the thing is, a lot of these things are not urgent in the moment. Sometimes they’re not even actual problems yet. But they feel urgent in my head. Like a small issue doesn’t stay small — if I make one mistake on something, my brain immediately treats it like it could set off this whole chain reaction and derail my entire future. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real, like I have to solve everything right then or I’m going to fall apart somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even for short periods during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think hopeless is the word I’d use, at least not in a clear way. I do have moments where I feel kind of discouraged or flat, especially if I’ve been thinking too much about everything I need to handle, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as feeling depressed, exactly.\n\nAs far as losing interest in things, I mean… sometimes I don’t feel like doing much, but it’s hard for me to tell whether that’s because I’ve lost interest or because I’m just tired and mentally drained. Like if a friend suggests something after work, I might think, “That sounds like too much,” even if normally I’d enjoy seeing them. But I don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things, because if I actually get myself there, I can sometimes still have an okay time.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a strong pattern of feeling hopeless or not caring about anything. It’s more that my mood can dip for a bit when I’m overwhelmed, but I’m not sure I’d call it that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you take less pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for short periods during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I’d say “less pleasure,” exactly. I’ve thought about that a little because it’s hard to separate enjoyment from just being tired or preoccupied.\n\nLike, there are things I normally like — watching a show, going out to eat with a friend, doing something low-key after work — and sometimes they don’t sound appealing in the moment. But it’s not always because I don’t enjoy them anymore. It’s more like the effort of starting feels like a lot, or I’m already mentally somewhere else, so I’m not as excited about it as I think I should be.\n\nIf I actually do the thing, I can still enjoy it at least somewhat. Maybe not always fully, because my mind can be distracted, but I don’t think I’ve had this clear sense of, “Nothing is enjoyable anymore.” It’s more inconsistent than that. Some moments I’m engaged and fine, and other times I’m just sort of going through the motions because I’m worn out. So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a definite loss of pleasure, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel down or sad most of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going on to explain it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of being sad most of the day for no clear reason. I mean, I do have times where I feel kind of low or emotionally worn down, but usually when I really think about it, it’s tied to feeling overwhelmed or like I’ve been carrying too much in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up and the whole day just feels sad for no reason, if that makes sense. More often it’s like my mood gets pulled down after I’ve been thinking about responsibilities or things I’m worried I’m not handling well. And then I might feel discouraged for a while, or quieter than usual, or like I don’t have much energy to be upbeat. But I wouldn’t say it’s most of the day, and I wouldn’t say it comes out of nowhere.\n\nI guess I’m a little unsure where the line is, because sometimes the reason isn’t one obvious event — it’s more like a buildup. But no, I haven’t really noticed days where I’m just sad most of the time without something behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling asleep because your thoughts are racing or you can't shut them off, even when you're physically tired?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll be physically exhausted — like, I can feel that my body is tired — and I’ll get into bed in my apartment, close my laptop, lights off or mostly off, and then my brain just keeps going. It’s not even always one big dramatic thought. It’s more like I start mentally rechecking everything: work deadlines, emails I might have missed, whether I handled something correctly, then tuition payments, then class assignments, then I remember some other thing I need to do, and it just keeps looping.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ll tell myself, “Okay, you’re done for the day, you need to sleep,” but it doesn’t feel like there’s an off switch. I can be lying there for hours, not doing anything, not even on my computer anymore, but still kind of working in my head. And then even when I do finally fall asleep, it doesn’t feel restful. I wake up feeling like I never really turned off, like my mind was still running in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any tightness in your muscles, especially in your neck, shoulders, or back, even when you're not doing anything physically strenuous?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I know what you mean in a general sense, like carrying tension in your shoulders or clenching without realizing it, but I haven’t really tracked that carefully.\n\nI do sometimes notice I’m sort of… held stiffly, I guess, like I’ll realize I’m sitting at my desk with my shoulders up or my posture is really rigid. But I don’t know if I’d call it muscle tightness as a symptom, exactly, because I also sit at a computer a lot for work, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just from being at my desk versus something else.\n\nMy neck and shoulders can feel tense at times, but I haven’t had this clear ongoing back or neck pain that I’ve been focused on. It’s more like I notice it after the fact, like, “Oh, I’m not relaxed,” but I wouldn’t say I’ve been doing anything physically strenuous when it happens. I just haven’t paid enough attention to say how often it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling asleep because your thoughts are racing or you can't shut them off, even when you're physically tired?\n\nPatient: Yes — definitely. Honestly, that’s one of the biggest reasons I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll be lying in bed in my apartment, physically tired, laptop already closed, trying to be done for the day, and my mind just keeps going. I start rechecking things in my head — work deadlines, whether I missed something, tuition payments, class assignments — and it just loops. It’s like I can’t convince my brain that it’s allowed to stop.\n\nSometimes I’ll be there for hours, not even doing anything, just lying there thinking through the same things again and again. And then even when I do finally sleep, I wake up feeling like I never really turned off. It doesn’t feel restful.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively sleepy or struggle to stay awake during the day, even after what you think should have been a full night's sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I don’t know if I’d describe it as, like, literally nodding off at my desk all the time, but I feel really drained during the day. By midafternoon at the office, it’s like I hit this wall where even small things feel weirdly hard — answering emails, responding to messages, getting through whatever task I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nAnd it’s not just “I could use a coffee” tired. It feels like my whole body and brain are running on empty. Even walking to the subway after work can feel like this huge effort, which sounds dramatic, but that’s how it feels in the moment.\n\nI keep thinking if I could just catch up on sleep or rest on the weekend, I’d bounce back, but that doesn’t really happen. I’ll try to take it easy, and then Monday comes and I still feel like I’m starting the week already depleted. So yes, during the day I’m tired a lot — more drained than sleepy maybe, but definitely struggling to feel awake and functional.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to get started on tasks or responsibilities, even when you know you need to do them, because it feels like there’s no motivation or energy to begin?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not even always that I don’t care about the task — like, I know the email needs an answer, or I know I need to start something for work — but by midafternoon at the office I feel so completely drained that starting feels weirdly impossible.\n\nEven small things can feel like they require this huge amount of effort. I’ll be sitting there looking at my inbox thinking, “Okay, just answer this one message,” and it still feels like I have to force myself through mud to begin. And then by the time I’m leaving work, even something basic like walking to the subway can feel like more effort than it should.\n\nI keep hoping that if I rest over the weekend, I’ll sort of reset and come back with more energy, but it doesn’t really work that way. I might take it easy, but I don’t feel restored enough. It’s like I’m starting from empty again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving more slowly than usual, or having trouble thinking clearly and making decisions, even when you're not physically tired?\n\nPatient: Yes — mostly the thinking clearly and making decisions part. I’m not sure I’d say I’m physically moving in slow motion all the time, but mentally I get really stuck.\n\nLike I’ll be looking at the same financial model at work, something I technically know how to review, and I’ll just… stare at it. I’ll read the same numbers or assumptions over and over and realize I haven’t actually taken anything in. Or with class readings, I’ll get through a paragraph and then notice I’ve basically reread the same paragraph five times and still couldn’t tell you what it said.\n\nAnd it’s not because the material is impossible. It’s more like my mind is already somewhere else, running through everything that could go wrong — what if I miss something in the model, what if I fall behind, what if I don’t understand the reading well enough — and then I’m technically looking at the task, but I’m not really there. Even small decisions can take way longer than they should because I keep second-guessing what the “right” choice is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes. Honestly, that’s probably the main thing that’s been going on. It’s not just one worry that comes and goes — my mind keeps jumping between different things, and it feels constant.

Like I’ll start out worrying that I’m falling behind at work, that I’m missing something important or not keeping up the way I’m supposed to. Then it turns into worrying about school and whether I’m wasting money on it, or whether I made the wrong choice trying to do all of this. It’s like every small issue feels bigger than it probably is. One email I haven’t answered, one assignment I’m unsure about, one expense I didn’t plan for — my brain treats it like it could derail everything, like my whole future is suddenly at risk. I know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real. So yes, more days than not, for a long time now, I’m worrying about multiple things at once.

I mostly notice the thoughts and the inability to settle down more than specific body symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or unable to relax, even when there’s no specific reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly, because I don’t always know what counts as “no specific reason.” Like, if I’m restless, my mind usually finds a reason pretty quickly, even if it’s kind of vague or not actually urgent.\n\nI do have times where I feel like I should be able to just sit down and relax, and I can’t really settle into it. I’ll keep dragging myself back to the task, and then a minute later I’m off in my head again, going through all these scenarios. Like things feel harder to tolerate, and I may have less patience for interruptions or unexpected requests. What if someone realizes I’m not doing enough?” And then somehow that turns into worrying about school, like whether I’m wasting money on it or whether I made the wrong decision, and then that turns into worrying about my family thinking I can’t handle things or that I’m not as put together as I should be at 29.\n\nAnd the thing is, a lot of these things are not urgent in the moment. Sometimes they’re not even actual problems yet. But they feel urgent in my head. Like a small issue doesn’t stay small — if I make one mistake on something, my brain immediately treats it like it could set off this whole chain reaction and derail my entire future. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real, like I have to solve everything right then or I’m going to fall apart somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even for short periods during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think hopeless is the word I’d use, at least not in a clear way. Like if a friend suggests something after work, I might think, “That sounds like too much,” even if normally I’d enjoy seeing them. But I don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things, because if I actually get myself there, I can sometimes still have an okay time.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed a strong pattern of feeling hopeless or not caring about anything. It’s more that my mood can dip for a bit when I’m overwhelmed, but I’m not sure I’d call it that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you take less pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for short periods during the day?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I’d say “less pleasure,” exactly. I’ve thought about that a little because it’s hard to separate enjoyment from just being tired or preoccupied.\n\nLike, there are things I normally like — watching a show, going out to eat with a friend, doing something low-key after work — and sometimes they don’t sound appealing in the moment. But it’s not always because I don’t enjoy them anymore. It’s more like the effort of starting feels like a lot, or I’m already mentally somewhere else, so I’m not as excited about it as I think I should be.\n\nIf I actually do the thing, I can still enjoy it at least somewhat. Maybe not always fully, because my mind can be distracted, but I don’t think I’ve had this clear sense of, “Nothing is enjoyable anymore.” It’s more inconsistent than that. Some moments I’m engaged and fine, and other times I’m just sort of going through the motions because I’m worn out. So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed a definite loss of pleasure, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you feel down or sad most of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going on to explain it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in the sense of being sad most of the day for no clear reason. I mean, I do have times where I feel kind of low or emotionally worn down, but usually when I really think about it, it’s tied to feeling overwhelmed or like I’ve been carrying too much in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up and the whole day just feels sad for no reason, if that makes sense. More often it’s like my mood gets pulled down after I’ve been thinking about responsibilities or things I’m worried I’m not handling well. And then I might feel discouraged for a while, or quieter than usual, or like I don’t have much energy to be upbeat. But I wouldn’t say it’s most of the day, and I wouldn’t say it comes out of nowhere.\n\nI guess I’m a little unsure where the line is, because sometimes the reason isn’t one obvious event — it’s more like a buildup. But no, I haven’t really noticed days where I’m just sad most of the time without something behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble falling asleep because your thoughts are racing or you can't shut them off, even when you're physically tired?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I’m here, because my sleep has basically fallen apart.\n\nI’ll be physically exhausted — like, I can feel that my body is tired — and I’ll get into bed in my apartment, close my laptop, lights off or mostly off, and then my brain just keeps going. It’s not even always one big dramatic thought. It’s more like I start mentally rechecking everything: work deadlines, emails I might have missed, whether I handled something correctly, then tuition payments, then class assignments, then I remember some other thing I need to do, and it just keeps looping.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ll tell myself, “Okay, you’re done for the day, you need to sleep,” but it doesn’t feel like there’s an off switch. I wake up feeling like I never really turned off, like my mind was still running in the background the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any tightness in your muscles, especially in your neck, shoulders, or back, even when you're not doing anything physically strenuous?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I might take it easy, but I don’t feel restored enough. Even small decisions can take way longer than they should because I keep second-guessing what the “right” choice is.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying about multiple things in your life, like work, school, or personal responsibilities, for more days than not over the past six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s probably the main thing that’s been going on. It’s not just one worry that comes and goes — my mind keeps jumping between different things, and it feels constant.\n\nLike I’ll start out worrying that I’m falling behind at work, that I’m missing something important or not keeping up the way I’m supposed to. Then it turns into worrying about school and whether I’m wasting money on it, or whether I made the wrong choice trying to do all of this. And then somehow it becomes about my family and whether they’re going to think I can’t handle my life or that I’m not as capable as I should be.\n\nIt’s like every small issue feels bigger than it probably is. One email I haven’t answered, one assignment I’m unsure about, one expense I didn’t plan for — my brain treats it like it could derail everything, like my whole future is suddenly at risk. I know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels very real. So yes, more days than not, for a long time now, I’m worrying about multiple things at once."
  }
]
